Answered by `worktree_removed` or `remove_worktree_failed`. |
| `watch_worktree {path, base}` | app → service | 0 | Watch this worktree of a followed project for the files panel, instead of any other (one at a time); its `changes` are listed against `base` (as `list_changes`). Refused with `error` ("<path> is not a worktree of a followed project"). |
| `unwatch_worktree` | app → service | 0 | Stop watching (the files panel and the `@` list closed). |
| `expand_ignored {path, folders}` | app → service | 0 | The ignored folders open in the Files tree of the watched worktree `path` (relative, no trailing `/`), replacing the last ones sent. Refused with `error` as `watch_worktree` is ("<path> is not a worktree of a followed project", a group's folder too, 14.1); nothing when another worktree is watched. While a folder is one that `files` lists in `ignored`, one level of it is listed and it is watched (see [Files panel watch](#files-panel-watch-31-31)). Answered by `files` (then `changes`) after the re-list. |
| `worktree_status {path, status}` | service → app | 0 | A worktree's new status, sent only when it differs from the last one sent (see [Worktree health](#worktree-health)); `status` is null when git could not tell. |
| `view {terminal, focused, visible}` | app → service | 0 | The terminal shown (null when none is, or a file is), whether the app window has the focus and whether it is visible (the page's `visibilityState`; false while hidden or minimised). Sent when one changes and after every `welcome` (`followView` in `src/follow.ts`; focus from `watchFocus`, visibility from `watchVisible` in `src/window.ts`). The worktree health check waits while the window is hidden (see [Worktree health](#worktree-health)). Answered by an `agent_state` for an agent waiting for you in that terminal that this makes seen. See [Agent states](#agent-states) item 5 and [Notifications](#notifications-hivemd-item-5) item 1. |
| `files {path, files, ignored, truncated}` | service → app | 0 | Every file git lists in the watched worktree `path` (tracked, and untracked but not ignored), sorted `/`-separated relative paths. `ignored` (14.2): what git ignores, sorted: files, and folders as `dir/` (shown closed and dimmed), with one level of each folder of `expand_ignored` (its folders as `dir/sub/`). Sent right after `watch_worktree` and after every change that alters the list (debounced). `truncated` when the list hit the cap (50 000 entries or 3 MiB of names, `files` first, then `ignored`). A listing that fails (e.g. the worktree was removed) is sent as `error`. |
| `stage_files {worktree, paths, staged}` | app → service | 0 | Stages (`git add`) or unstages (`git reset`, which works before the first commit too) `paths` of `worktree` (a worktree of a followed git project, 27.6): 1 to 10,000 paths relative to it, at most 4096 bytes each, never leaving it; given to git on stdin as literal pathspecs. Unstaging a staged rename unstages its old path too. Answered by `git_done` or `git_failed`; the files watch sends the `changes`. |
| `commit {worktree, message}` | app → service | 0 | `git commit --message=<message>` in `worktree` (the app asked first): what is staged, with the space's git identity (21.5), the repository's hooks run as git runs them (never skipped), within 5 minutes. The message is 1 to 16 KiB (one argument, within Windows' command line), not blank. Answered by `git_done` ("Committed <short id>: <subject>") or `git_failed` (git's own output and errors, hooks' included, cut at 4 KiB). |
| `get_push_target {worktree}` | app → service | 0 | Where `push` would push the branch of `worktree`; answered by `push_target`. |
| `push_target {worktree, branch, target, at, set_upstream, ahead, error}` | service → app | 0 | The branch and where it goes: its upstream (`target` as `<remote>/<branch>`, to show; `at` the `{remote, merge}` exactly, the remote's name or URL and its `refs/heads/<name>`), or with none `origin/<branch>` with `set_upstream`; `ahead` its commits the remote-tracking branch has not (null when unknown). `error` (and the rest null) when it cannot be pushed: a detached HEAD, an upstream that is a local branch or not a branch, no upstream and no `origin`. |
| `push {worktree, branch, at}` | app → service | 0 | Refused when the branch, its remote or its ref (`push_target`'s `branch` and `at`, as the user confirmed them, compared exactly, never as the shown `target`) changed since. `git push [--set-upstream] -- <remote> refs/heads/<branch>:<upstream ref>` in `worktree` (the app asked first), never forced (no `+`, no force option exists), with the space's identity and GitHub account's token (credentials stay git's and `gh`'s, 9.30), never prompting, within 5 minutes. Answered by `git_done` ("Pushed <branch> to <target>") or `git_failed` (a rejected push is git's message). |
| `git_done {worktree, action, message}` | service → app | 0 | A git action of the Changes panel worked: `action` is `stage`, `unstage`, `commit` or `push`; `message` is shown as is. |
| `git_failed {worktree, action, message}` | service → app | 0 | It failed: git's message, or why it was refused. |
| `list_changes {path, base}` | app → service | 0 | What changed in `path`, a worktree of a followed project, against `base`: `"head"`, `"branch"` (the merge-base with the main worktree's branch), `{"turn":{"session"}}` (the agent's last turn, 27.2) or `{"commit":{"id"}}` (a chosen commit, 27.2), see [Changes](#changes); answered by `changes`. |
| `list_commits {path}` | app → service | 0 | The commits of `path`, a worktree of a followed project, to compare with (27.2); answered by `commits`. |
| `changes {path, base, branch, base_error, files, added, removed, error, turns}` | service → app | 0 | Every file that differs from the base (see [Changes](#changes)), sorted by path. `base` is the base used (as asked, or `head`); `branch` the main worktree's branch this worktree can be compared with (null for the main worktree, or when that one is detached, or when the path is refused); `base_error` why the base asked is not used but `head` is ("No branch to compare with: this is the main worktree, or it is detached", "No commit in common with <branch>", "This commit is not in the repository", "No snapshot of this agent's last turn in this worktree", "The snapshot of this turn is no longer complete: …", or git's error). `turns` (27.2, optional, empty from an older service) = `[{session, title, started_ms, late}]`, the agents whose last turn's snapshot is of this worktree, the latest first (`late`: the snapshot finished after the agent's first edit). `files` = `[{path, status, old_path, added, removed, seen?, staged}]`, `status` one of `added`, `modified`, `deleted`, `renamed`, `untracked`; `old_path` is a rename's source; `staged` (27.6) what of it the next commit takes, `no`, `partly` or `yes` (from `git status`'s index side), null for a file with nothing left to commit (only committed since the base); `added`/`removed` are line counts, null for a binary file; `seen` (27.1, left out when the file has no mark) `seen` or `changed` (changed since seen, see [Seen files](#seen-files-271)). `added`/`removed` at the top are the totals of every file. `error` says why nothing could be listed (e.g. "<path> is not a worktree of a followed project"), or that the list was cut short. Also sent, unasked, after every refresh of the watched worktree (see [Files panel watch](#files-panel-watch-31-31)). |
| `mark_seen {path, base, files, seen}` | app → service | 0 | Marks `files` (paths of `path`'s `changes`; others are ignored) as seen in its diff against `base` (`seen` true), or not (false), see [Seen files](#seen-files-271). Answered by `changes` (the same as `list_changes`), or by `error` ("Cannot mark the files as seen: …") when the marks cannot be saved (the file's cap). |
| `list_comments {path}` | app → service | 0 | The review comments of `path`, a worktree of a followed project or a folder project's folder (27.3), each first moved to where its code is now (see [Review comments](#review-comments-273)); answered by `comments`, or `error` ("Cannot list the review comments: <path> is not a worktree of a followed project"). |
| `add_comment {path, file, from, to, body}` | app → service | 0 | A comment `body` on the lines `from`–`to` (1-based, inclusive) of `file` in `path` as the file is on disk now. Answered by `comments`, or `error` "Cannot add the comment: …" (no text, over 4000 characters, over 1000 lines, a line past the file, 500 comments already, the file missing, over 2 MiB or 50 000 lines, a path out of the worktree, `comments.json` full). |
| `resolve_comment {path, id, resolved}` | app → service | 0 | Resolves the comment `id` of `path`, or opens it again (`resolved` false); an unknown id changes nothing. Answered by `comments`. |
| `delete_comment {path, id}` | app → service | 0 | Deletes the comment `id` of `path`. Answered by `comments`. |
| `comments {path, comments}` | service → app | 0 | The review comments of `path`, oldest first: `[{id, file, from, to, body, code, before, after, author, ms, resolved, outdated}]`. `from`/`to` are where the code is now (its last place when `outdated`); `code` the text of its lines (the first 20, each cut at 200 characters), `before`/`after` up to 3 lines each side; `author` the service's user (`USER`, "you" without one); `ms` when it was written. Sent when asked, after `add_comment`, `resolve_comment` and `delete_comment`, and after every refresh of the watched worktree while it has comments (before its `changes`). |
| `list_scripts {path}` | app → service | 0 | The scripts of `path`, a worktree of a followed project (23.4); answered by `scripts`, or refused with `error` ("<path> is not a worktree of a followed project", a group's folder too). The answer is remembered as the scripts the app has: see [Project scripts and ports](#project-scripts-and-ports-68). |
| `commits {path, commits, truncated, error}` | service → app | 0 | The last 500 commits of the worktree's `HEAD` (27.2): `[{id, short, author, time_ms, subject, on_branch}]`, those made on its branch since it left the main worktree's branch first (`on_branch`), then the older ones, each newest first; `author` clipped to 100 characters, `subject` to 200; `truncated` when more were left out; `error` why none could be listed. |
| `scripts {path, scripts, problems, port}` | service → app | 0 | The scripts the user can run in `path` (23.4): `scripts` = `[{name, command, source}]`, the project's run scripts from the settings first (`source` `settings`), then the repository's (`package_json`, `taskfile`, `makefile`) that no settings script of the same name replaces; `command` is the line Hive types into a new terminal there. `problems`: one line per file not read or cut short ("Makefile: a link or not a regular file, not read"). `port`: the first port of the worktree's block (given one if it has none), null when no block can be saved. Sent when asked, and unasked for the worktree last asked for when its scripts change: after a change while it is the watched worktree, and after the settings are saved. |
| `list_sessions` | app → service | 0 | Claude Code's sessions of the current space's projects, in every Claude account's folder (12.2) (the Sessions tab, when it opens and for another space; D19.4: no more polling, the service pushes changes); answered by `sessions`, unless the list is the one the app has. |
| `sessions {sessions, error, truncated}` | service → app | 0 | The sessions whose `cwd` lies in a followed worktree, the most recent first: `sessions` = `[{id, project, worktree, cwd, title, last_role, last_text, messages, model, branch, context_tokens, output_tokens, updated_ms, log, state, running, config_dir, resume_command, files_changed}]` (`files_changed`: how many files Hive recorded the session changing, 27.5; `config_dir`: the Claude config folder of the account whose folder holds its log, null for the default one's; `resume_command`: what "Copy Resume Command" copies, see [Accounts](#claude-accounts-122)), at most the 500 most recent (`truncated` when older ones were left out; they are not read). `error` says why the first Claude projects folder that could not be read was skipped (the others' sessions are still listed). Sent only when it differs from the last `sessions` the app got since it connected or last sent `list_projects` (a reloaded UI does). Also sent unasked once the app has a list (D19.4): when `hive::session_watch` sees a followed project's log or a record of a running `claude` change, and when an agent of Hive's terminals is detected or ends (`SessionStart`, `SessionEnd`, its terminal's exit). |
| `restore_sessions {sessions[{id, cwd, config_dir}]}` | service → app | 0 | Sent once, right after the first `welcome` since the service started, when the last app closed with sessions running in Hive's terminals (kept in `<data>/hive/open-sessions.json`, read once and removed): the app resumes each in a new terminal, with its terminal's Claude account (`config_dir`, null for the default one; a list kept before 12.2 has none: the default one). |
| `locate_session {id, target}` | app → service | 0 | Where Windows sees a listed session's `log` or working `folder` (`target`), to open or reveal it. Answered by `session_located`. |
| `session_located {id, target, windows_path, error}` | service → app | 0 | The path from `wslpath -w`, or why not (`error`; the log's untrusted `cwd` is checked as a worktree's folder is). |
| `delete_session {id}` | app → service | 0 | Delete a listed session's log and the folder of the same name beside it (its subagents' logs), refused while it runs. Answered by `session_deleted` or `delete_session_failed`. |
| `session_deleted {id}` | service → app | 0 | The session's log is gone. |
| `delete_session_failed {id, message}` | service → app | 0 | Nothing deleted; `message` is shown as is. |
| `move_session {id, config_dir}` | app → service | 0 | Move a listed session's log and the folder of the same name beside it into the projects folder of the Claude account `config_dir` (`null`: the default one), to resume it as that account (21.10, D22.5): a rename, else (another disk) a bounded copy written to disk, then the original removed. Refused while it runs (in Hive or outside), for a folder no account has, and when that account already has a log or folder of the session; its `file-history` stays behind. Answered by `session_moved` (also when it is there already) or `move_session_failed`. |
| `session_moved {id, config_dir}` | service → app | 0 | The session's log is in that account's folder: the app resumes it as that account. |
| `move_session_failed {id, message}` | service → app | 0 | Nothing moved; `message` is shown as is. |
| `who_wrote {worktree, path, line, side, base}` | app → service | 0 | Who wrote `line` (1-based) of `path` in a followed worktree (27.5, see [Line provenance](#line-provenance-275)): `side` `worktree` (the file now: the editor, the diff's new side) or `base` (the diff's old side, at `base`, `head` or `branch`). Answered by `line_author`. |
| `line_author {worktree, path, line, side, origin}` | service → app | 0 | `origin` is `{kind: "session", edit, commit}` (`edit` = `{session, title, deleted, project, worktree, agent, agent_type, tool, tool_use_id, ms, user_modified, start, len}`; `commit` null: followed live; else `{id, author, ms, summary}`, found through `git blame`, so only probable), `{kind: "editor", ms}` (the user, in Hive's editor) or `{kind: "unknown", commit}` (no session recorded; `commit` is the blamed one when committed). Advisory, never proof. |
| `list_session_files {id}` | app → service | 0 | The files Hive recorded the session `id` changing (27.5). Answered by `session_files`. |
| `session_files {id, files, truncated}` | service → app | 0 | `files` = `[{worktree, path, edits, lines_now, first_line, last_ms}]`, the most recently changed first, at most 500 (`truncated` when more): its recorded edits of the file, how many of its lines are still the session's as Hive last looked, the first of them, and the last edit's time. |
| `search_files {worktree, query}` | app → service | 0 | The lines of a followed worktree's files holding `query` (the sidebar's Files, "Contents"; `hive::search`): `git grep` as a fixed string, ignoring case, over tracked files and untracked ones that are not ignored, binary files skipped; `query` 1 to 256 bytes on one line. Answered by `search_results`. |
| `search_results {worktree, query, matches[{path, line, text}], truncated, error}` | service → app | 0 | In path order, at most 50 lines per file and 1000 in all (`truncated` when there were more); `text` cut at 300 characters. `error` says why nothing could be searched. |
| `open_file {worktree, path, base}` | app → service | 0 | One file of `worktree` (a worktree of a followed project); `path` is relative to it; `base` as in `list_changes` (the same fallback to `HEAD`). Answered by `file`. |
| `file {worktree, path, content, base, version, binary, too_large, error}` | service → app | 0 | The file as asked (see [Viewer and diff](#viewer-and-diff-33-31)): `content` is its text on disk (null when gone), `base` its text at the base asked (a rename's old path; null when new or before the first commit), `version` an opaque token for the bytes on disk (null when they were not read), `binary` / `too_large` leave both texts null, `error` says why it could not be read (e.g. "<path> is not a worktree of a followed project", "a.txt does not exist", "not a relative path inside the worktree"). `media` (24.2, optional): `{mime, token, version}` when the file on disk is an image or a PDF by its bytes, or an HTML page by its name (24.4, `text/html; charset=utf-8`), within its cap; the Tauri pump adds `url`, its `hive-file:` URL (see [Viewer and diff](#viewer-and-diff-33-31)). |
| `read_file {id, token, path, offset, len}` | app → service | 0 | The app's `hive-file:` scheme (24.2): at most `len` (and 1 MiB, `READ_LIMIT`) bytes from `offset` of `path` of the worktree `token` names (`media.token`), the worktree checked followed and the path checked as `open_file`'s on every read. Answered by `file_range` with the same `id`, then, unless it has an `error`, a data frame on channel `id`. |
| `file_range {id, size, mime, version, error}` | service → app | 0 | The file's whole `size`, its type read from its first 4096 bytes (`application/octet-stream` when not an image or a PDF) and `version` (its size and modification time); `error` says why nothing was read (e.g. "a.png does not exist", "a.png is larger than 20 MiB", "no worktree has this token"). Consumed by the Tauri side, never forwarded to the UI. |
| `open_table {worktree, path}` | app → service | 0 | A CSV or TSV file (23.6) or an `.xlsx` / `.xls` / `.ods` workbook (23.7), by its extension, of `worktree` as a table, on disk; the path rules of `open_file`. Answered by `table`. |
| `table {worktree, path, sheets, error}` | service → app | 0 | The file's sheets (see [Viewer and diff](#viewer-and-diff-33-31)), each `{name, rows, total, partial}`: one with an empty `name` for a CSV or TSV file (a workbook, 23.7, one per sheet); `rows` its first rows, each its cells as text (rows may differ in length); `total` how many rows it has; `partial` when it was counted only up to the service's cap. No sheets when `error` says why (as `file`'s, "not a table file", "not a readable workbook: …", "the workbook is larger than 32 MiB" or "the workbook could not be read"). |
| `edit_table {worktree, path, content, edit}` | app → service | 0 | `edit` on the table of `content`, the text of the CSV or TSV file `path` being edited (25.4); `edit` is `{op, ...}`: `set_cell {row, column, value}`, `insert_row` / `remove_row` / `insert_column` / `remove_column {at}`, or null for that text's table as it is. Nothing is read or written on disk (see [Editing](#editing-35-31), item 12). Answered by `table_edited`. |
| `table_edited {worktree, path, content, sheet, error}` | service → app | 0 | The text after the edit and its table; both null when `error` says why nothing changed: the worktree is not followed, not a table file, `content` over 1 MiB, a cell not in the table, a table not read whole, or an answer that would not fit one frame. |
| `save_file {worktree, path, content, version}` | app → service | 0 | Write `content` over the file (see [Editing](#editing-35-31)), only if its bytes on disk still have `version`; `version` null means the file must not exist (it is created). Answered by `file_saved` or `save_failed`. |
| `file_saved {worktree, path, version}` | service → app | 0 | The file now holds the saved text; `version` is its new token. |
| `save_failed {worktree, path, error, message}` | service → app | 0 | Nothing was written. `error` is `conflict` (the bytes on disk are not `version`, or are over 1 MiB), `too_large` (`content` over 1 MiB), `invalid_path` (not a followed worktree, or the path rules of `open_file`) or `io`; `message` is shown as is. |
| `create_file {worktree, folder, name}` | app → service | 0 | Create the empty file `name` in `folder` (relative to the worktree, `""` for its root; the path rules of `open_file`, resolved inside the worktree). `name` is one component: not empty, `.` or `..`, no `/`, `\` or NUL, at most 255 bytes. Never overwrites (`O_EXCL`, which also refuses a symlink). Answered by `file_created` (the app opens it as editable text unless that would drop unsaved edits) or `file_op_failed`. |
| `file_created {worktree, path}` | service → app | 0 | The file now exists at `path`, relative to the worktree (through a symlinked folder: where it resolves). |
| `rename_file {worktree, path, name}` | app → service | 0 | Rename the regular file or folder `path` (not following a symlink; never `.git` nor anything in it) to `name` (the rules of `create_file`; never `.git`) in the same folder, never over an existing entry. A file: a hard link to the new name, then the old name removed (the link removed again if that fails). A folder (9.1): one no-replace rename (`renameat2(RENAME_NOREPLACE)` on Linux, `renamex_np(RENAME_EXCL)` on macOS), refused while it is or holds a followed worktree ("it holds the worktree …") or the working directory of a process ("in use by bash (123): close its terminals first", `projects::held`). Answered by `file_renamed` or `file_op_failed`. |
| `move_file {worktree, path, folder}` | app → service | 0 | Move the regular file or folder `path` (the rules of `rename_file`) into `folder` (the rules of `create_file`'s `folder`, resolved inside the worktree), keeping its name, never over an existing entry, as `rename_file` does; a folder never into itself or one of its own folders ("a folder cannot go into itself"). Its own folder moves nothing (answered `file_renamed` with `to` = `path`). Answered by `file_renamed` or `file_op_failed`. |
| `create_folder {worktree, folder, name}` | app → service | 0 | Create the folder `name` (the rules of `create_file`'s `name`) in `folder` (the rules of `create_file`), mode 0755 less the umask, never over an existing entry (a dangling symlink included). Answered by `folder_created` or `file_op_failed`. |
| `file_renamed {worktree, path, to}` | service → app | 0 | `path` is now `to` (renamed or moved); for a folder, every path under `path` is now under `to`. The open files (their tabs, texts and edit buffers), the open/closed state of the folders under it and the tree's active row follow. The tree's rows follow through the watch's `files`. |
| `folder_created {worktree, path}` | service → app | 0 | The folder now exists at `path`, relative to the worktree (through a symlinked folder: where it resolves). The Files tree shows it even while empty (git lists no empty folder), with the folders around it open. |
| `delete_file {worktree, path}` | app → service | 0 | Delete the entry `path` (9.29): on macOS it moves to the Trash of its volume (`NSFileManager trashItemAtURL`, D18.10e), elsewhere it is removed for good, no trash; either way resolved as `rename_file`'s `path` (its folder inside the worktree; never the root, `.git` nor anything in it). A file or a symlink is removed itself, never what it points to. A folder goes with what it holds (`remove_dir_all` off macOS, which never follows a symlink inside it), refused while it is or holds a followed worktree or a process's working directory (as `rename_file`), when anything in it is named `.git` (another repository or worktree), and past 10 000 entries or 64 levels of folders under it ("… holds more than 10000 entries: delete it in a terminal", counted before anything is removed). Answered by `file_deleted` or `file_op_failed`. |
| `file_deleted {worktree, path}` | service → app | 0 | `path` (as the app sent it) is gone, with everything under it. The Files tree drops it and what it held at once (the watch's next `files` confirms it); the open files under it close their tabs, and those with unsaved edits ask first, in one question. |
| `file_op_failed {worktree, message}` | service → app | 0 | Nothing was created, renamed, moved or deleted; `message` shows as is in the file name dialog, or as an error toast when no dialog is open for the worktree (a drag). |
| `open_in_editor {worktree, path, reveal}` | app → service | 0 | Where Windows sees this file (an empty `path`: the worktree's folder), to open it with its default app, or, `reveal` (default `false`), this file or folder to show in the Explorer or the Finder (D19.1). Answered by `editor_target`. |
| `editor_target {worktree, path, reveal, windows_path, error}` | service → app | 0 | The file's Windows path (`wslpath -w`), or why not (`error`, e.g. an executable or a type the system might run). `reveal` as asked: a path checked only to be revealed, which the app side never opens. The app acts on it once, only while its own request for that `worktree`, `path` and `reveal` is pending (9.7). |
| `get_settings` | app → service | 0 | Answered by `settings`. |
| `resync` | app → service | 0 | The UI was reloaded over the same connection (18.21): the service sends every live agent again as after `welcome` (`agent_detected`, `agent_state`, `agent_title`, `agent_usage`), `subagent_worktrees` and the session usage. |
| `settings {settings}` | service → app | 0 | The service's settings (see [Settings](#settings)). Sent right after `welcome`, and in answer to `get_settings` and to a saved `set_settings`. |
| `set_settings {settings}` | app → service | 0 | Check and save the whole settings. Answered by `settings`, or by `settings_failed` with nothing saved (also when it removes an account a space uses, 22.5: "<account> is the Claude account of the space <space>: choose another one for it first", or a gh config folder a space's GitHub account is in, 24.13: "The GitHub account of the space <space> is in <folder>: choose another one for it first"). |
| `create_account {name}` | app → service | 0 | A new Claude account whose folder Hive makes (22.3, see [Claude accounts](#claude-accounts-122), item 8). Answered by `settings`, or by `settings_failed` with nothing made. |
| `remove_account {config_dir, delete_folder}` | app → service | 0 | Remove the Claude account whose folder is `config_dir`, as `set_settings` would (refused for an account a space uses, 22.5, before anything is deleted), then delete that folder when `delete_folder` and Hive made it (22.3). Answered by `settings`, then `settings_failed` when the folder could not be deleted; by `settings_failed` alone, with nothing removed, for an unknown account or a folder Hive did not make. |
| `open_settings_file` | app → service | 0 | Open the settings file in an editor; the service first writes the settings in use when there is no file. Answered by `editor_target` with an empty `worktree` and `path` (`windows_path` from `wslpath -w`; the path itself on macOS). |
| `get_diagnostics` | app → service | 0 | Answered by `diagnostics`. |
| `diagnostics {settings_file, wrapper, claude, git_error}` | service → app | 0 | For the settings' About section: the settings file, the `claude` wrapper and the `claude` it runs, as found on the user's `PATH` (see [The user's `claude`](#the-users-claude); null when none is), and why the service runs no git (Apple's Command Line Tools missing, 18.19; null when git runs, and About then has no git row). |
| `get_shared` | app → service | 0 | What the folder every Claude account shares holds (21.9, see [The shared plugin](#the-shared-plugin-219)), made first when missing. Answered by `shared`. |
| `shared {folder, plugin, skills, agents, commands, mcp_servers, error}` | service → app | 0 | For Settings → Accounts' "Shared" section: the folder, the plugin's name (its skills, agents and commands are `/<plugin>:<name>`), and the names of its skills (`skills/<name>/SKILL.md`), agents (`agents/<name>.md`), commands (`commands/<name>.md`) and MCP servers (`.mcp.json`'s `mcpServers` keys, or its top-level keys without that key), each sorted, at most 200, only names of at most 128 bytes that are not hidden and have no control or bidirectional-formatting character; `error`: why the folder could not be made, or `.mcp.json` was not understood (at most 256 KiB, a JSON object), else null. |
| `open_shared` | app → service | 0 | Open the shared folder in the Explorer or the Finder, made first when missing. Answered by `editor_target` with an empty `worktree` and the `path` `shared`. |
| `list_uninstall {keep_data, accounts, app}` | app → service | 0 | What uninstalling Hive would remove (23.5, see [Uninstall](#uninstall-235)). `app` is the app's own bundle, set by the app side from its executable's path (`hive_lib::app_bundle`), never by the UI; the service takes it only as an absolute path to a folder (not a link) named `*.app`. Answered by `uninstall_list`. |
| `uninstall_list {keep_data, accounts, items, account_folders}` | service → app | 0 | The choices as asked; `items` (`{path, kind}`, `kind` one of `data`, `account`, `program`, `cache`, `app`) is what `uninstall` with them removes, in order, the app last, only what exists; `account_folders` the account folders Hive made (22.3), whether they go or not, for the dialog's box. |
| `uninstall {keep_data, accounts, app}` | app → service | 0 | Uninstall Hive with these choices (as `list_uninstall`): the service stops serving and ignores every later request, ends every terminal, removes the list (to the Trash on macOS, deleted on Linux) and answers `uninstalled`, then ends when the app disconnects. |
| `uninstalled {failed}` | service → app | 0 | Hive is uninstalled; `failed` (`{path, error}`) is what could not go. The UI quits at once, or first lists the failures. |
| `settings_failed {message}` | service → app | 0 | Settings not saved (a value out of range, e.g. "agents.silence_secs must be between 2 and 60 (got 61)", or the write failed), or, right after `settings`, why the settings file was ignored ("Ignoring <file>: <why>. Using the defaults until the settings are saved."). Shown as is. |
| `error {message}` | service → client | 0 or n | A refused request, e.g. channel 0, a channel already open, a bad cwd, an unexpected message, or a second app. |

Between the app's Rust side and the WebView (#24), control messages travel on one Tauri `Channel` (given by the `connect` command) as the service's JSON plus a `channel` field, e.g. `{"type":"terminal_opened","channel":1}`. Terminal output travels as raw bytes on a separate `Channel` per terminal (given by `open_terminal`). No Tauri events are used. The Rust side forwards each control payload as it came, reading only its `type` (it acts on `welcome`, `version_mismatch` and `terminal_exited` itself), so it never decodes a message it only passes on. A payload that is not JSON or has no string `type` is skipped with a line on stderr; the connection and its terminals stay up (only a broken frame ends it). The Rust side adds `app_version` and `app_protocol` (its own values) to `version_mismatch`, so the UI can show both sides, and `bundled` (see [Bridge start](#bridge-start)), and one message of its own. A remote host's link (26.17, see [Remote hosts over ssh](#remote-hosts-over-ssh-2617)) adds `host` (its alias) to each message it forwards and to `host_link`; the local service's messages carry none:

| Message | Direction | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `disconnected {reason, bundled}` | app (Rust) → UI | The bridge exited or its stdout closed. `reason` is the bridge's stderr (at most 16 KiB), a protocol error, or "the hive bridge exited"; `bundled` as on `version_mismatch`. Every open terminal gets `terminal_exited {code: null}` first. Not sent after `version_mismatch`, whose connection still ends every open terminal (`terminal_exited {code: null}` after it) and keeps nothing of what it told for the next one (26.17). |
| `app_mode {mode, wsl}` | app (Rust) → UI | Only where the app offers WSL or Windows (see [App mode](#app-mode-1254)), first on every `connect`: `mode` is `"wsl"`, `"native"` or null (not chosen yet: nothing starts), `wsl` whether WSL has a distribution. The UI then waits for a new connection. |
| `translocated {notice}` | app (Rust) → UI | Only on macOS, when the app runs translocated (17.10): its executable's path has an `AppTranslocation` component (`hive_lib::translocated(macos, current_exe)`, false off macOS or without a path; `main.rs` only calls it), i.e. macOS runs a quarantined app opened where it was downloaded from a random read-only copy the updater cannot replace. Sent first on every `connect`; `notice` is true only for the run's first one, which opens "Move Hive to Applications" (`src/shell/TranslocatedDialog.tsx`: quit, move to Applications, `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine /Applications/Hive.app`, reopen). While translocated the update button opens that dialog instead of installing, and the Rust side never runs the installer (`install_update` answers `update_failed`; the exit installs nothing). |
| `notification_clicked {agent}` | app (Rust) → UI | The user clicked the OS notification about `agent` (13.5); the window is already in front. See [Notifications](#notifications-hivemd-item-5) item 3. |
| `quit_requested` | app (Rust) → UI | macOS asked to quit (⌘Q, the Dock's Quit, logout) while agents would end and `confirm_close` is on (17.7); the app stayed and its window came up. The UI asks as the window's Close does (see [Closing the app](#closing-the-app-18) item 5). |
| `notification_permission {permission}` | app (Rust) → UI | The answer to the app command `check_notifications` (17.19): `granted`, `denied` or `unknown` (the app cannot ask, or the user has not answered). See [Notifications](#notifications-hivemd-item-5) item 5. |
| `terminal_permissions {screen_recording, accessibility}` | app (Rust) → UI | The answer to the app command `check_terminal_permissions` (25.5): each `granted`, `denied` (not granted) or `unknown` (the app cannot read them). See [Permissions of programs in terminals](#permissions-of-programs-in-terminals-255). |
| `menu {item}` | app (Rust) → UI | A click on one of the macOS app menu's own items (17.13), e.g. `settings`; the UI runs that item's action (see [The app menu](#the-app-menu-1713)). |
| `update_checked {error}` | app (Rust) → UI | A check for updates the user asked for (`check_update {manual: true}`, the menu's "Check for Updates…") found nothing to offer: `error` null when Hive is up to date, else why the check or the download failed. |
| `tray_clicked {agent}` | app (Rust) → UI | The user chose `agent` in the menu bar icon's menu (17.22); the window is already in front. See [Notifications](#notifications-hivemd-item-5) item 7. |
| `terminal_kept {channel, cwd, worktree, account}` | app (Rust) → UI | A terminal kept through a WebContent crash (18.21), one per open terminal in channel order, right after the replayed `welcome`: `cwd` is what `open_terminal` got, `worktree` and `account` its `terminal_opened`'s (null before it). The UI reattaches to it (see [App connect](#app-connect-app-side) item 4). Also, after the service's `terminals_kept` (26.16), for each kept terminal the app does not have (the app restarted): the UI opens its tab under its worktree and takes its ring (see [Reattach](#reattach-after-a-dropped-link-2616)). |
| `terminal_reattached {channel}` | app (Rust) → UI | 26.16: a terminal the UI already shows was attached again after a reconnect (`attach_terminal` with the bytes it received); the UI redraws it: a resize one column wider and back, so a full-screen program (Claude Code) draws itself again. |
| `host_link {host, state, …}` | app (Rust) → UI | A remote host's link changed (26.17), replayed to a reloaded UI: `connecting`; `installing {cpu}` (uploading this app's Linux build); `connected`; `reconnecting {reason, retry_secs}`; `disconnected {reason, kind}` with `kind` `host_key`, `auth`, `platform`, `install`, `version` (with `version`, `app_version` and `terminals`, Q8: the UI offers `restart_remote` or staying disconnected) `network` (the ladder ran out) or `limit` (the remote went past one of its link's caps, 26.19); `removed` (the host left the settings). Every terminal of a link that stops gets `terminal_exited {code: null}` first. |
| `files_dropped {text, x, y}` | app (Rust) → UI | Files dropped from Finder on the window at (`x`, `y`), points (CSS pixels) from its top left (18.1, macOS); `text` is their paths, shell-quoted, each followed by a space. The UI types it into the terminal whose pane is there, or ignores it. See [Terminals in the UI](#terminals-in-the-ui-xtermjs) item 4. |

A project is `{id, name, path, worktrees, error}`: `id` and `path` are the main worktree's path, `name` its folder name, and `error` (or null) says why `git worktree list` failed, e.g. for a moved folder. A worktree is `{id, name, path, branch, main, claude, status}`: `id` is its path, `branch` is null when detached, `main` marks the main worktree and `claude` one in `<repo>/.claude/worktrees/`. `name` is the folder name for a Claude worktree and the branch otherwise (the folder name when detached). `status` is `{changes, added, removed, ahead, behind, merged, last_commit_ms}` or null (see [Worktree health](#worktree-health)). The app never derives any of these.

`AgentEvent` (internal to the service, never sent to the app) has these fields: `provider`, `terminal_id`, `session_id`, `subagent {id, agent_type, teammate_name}`, `cwd`, `kind`, `activity` (below), and `raw` (the unchanged payload).
`kind` is one of: `session_started`, `prompt_submitted`, `tool_started`/`tool_finished`/`tool_failed {tool}`, `permission_requested {tool}`, `notification {notification}`, `turn_finished`, `turn_failed {error}`, `subagent_started`, `subagent_stopped`, `teammate_idled` (`TeammateIdle`), `compact_started` (`PreCompact`), `compact_finished` (`PostCompact`), `elicitation_requested` (`Elicitation`), `session_ended {reason}`, `worktree_created {name, path}`, `worktree_removed {path}`, or `other {event}`.
`notification` is one of `permission_prompt`, `elicitation_dialog`, `elicitation_url_dialog`, `elicitation_complete`, `elicitation_response`, `idle_prompt`, `agent_needs_input`, `quota_auto_resume_fired`, `quota_auto_resume_stale`, `quota_auto_resume_disabled`, or `other`.
`activity` is set on `tool_started` and `permission_requested` only: a short text from the payload's untrusted `tool_input` (`hive::adapter`): Edit/Write/MultiEdit/NotebookEdit "Editing <file>" and Read "Reading <file>", the file relative to the payload's `cwd` when inside it; Bash its `description`, else the first line of its `command`; Grep/Glob "Searching <pattern>"; Agent/Task its `description`; AskUserQuestion "Asking a question"; ExitPlanMode "Plan ready for approval"; any other tool, or a missing field, the tool's name. `compact_started` has the activity "Compacting". Control characters are dropped and it is cut at 120 characters.

## Sequences

### Bridge start
1. The app runs `wsl.exe [-d $HIVE_WSL_DISTRO] --exec /bin/sh -c <BRIDGE_SCRIPT> sh "$HIVE_BRIDGE" "<bundled>"` (`src-tauri/src/lib.rs`). `--exec` skips the user's login shell, so no fish config runs. On macOS (5.2) the app runs the same script natively: `/bin/sh -c <BRIDGE_SCRIPT> sh "$HIVE_BRIDGE" "<bundled>"` (`HIVE_WSL_DISTRO` is ignored). The script is a constant; both values are separate arguments, empty when absent. The script runs, in order:
   - `$HIVE_BRIDGE`, an absolute Linux path (development, `scripts/win-dev.sh`);
   - the `hive` the installer bundles (4.18): `<bundled>` is its Windows path in the app's resources, only when that file exists. a POSIX path (macOS) is used as is, else `wslpath -u` (after dropping a verbatim `\\?\` prefix) finds it in WSL; when `cmp` finds it differs from `<data>/hive/bin/hive` (`$HIVE_HOME/bin/hive` when `HIVE_HOME` is absolute, else `~/.hive/bin/hive`; the folders made with `umask 077`), it is copied to `hive.new` there, loses its `com.apple.quarantine` attribute when `xattr` exists (macOS; a missing or failing `xattr` is ignored) and is renamed over it, so a running `hive` keeps its file. On macOS the copy also gives hooks a stable path, since an app opened from Downloads runs from a random translocated one. The copy runs, so hooks (which call the daemon's `current_exe`) use it too;
   - otherwise `~/.cargo/bin/hive` (`cargo install`, development).

   `bridge_command` also tells whether the bridge runs the bundled `hive`: true when that file exists and `HIVE_BRIDGE` is empty, i.e. an installed app (12.4). `main.rs` hands it to `Hive::with_bundled`, and Rust adds it as `bundled` to `version_mismatch` and `disconnected`, so the connection dialog never tells an installed app's user to run `cargo`.

   In native mode on Windows (see [App mode](#app-mode-1254)) the app runs `hive.exe bridge` itself (`native_bridge`): the `hive.exe` bundled next to the app when that file exists (then `bundled` is true), else `$HIVE_BRIDGE` (a Windows path), else `hive.exe` on `PATH` (`cargo install`). No script: the install folder is already a stable path. The bridge then starts the service from its own copy in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\hive\bin` (12.5.5), so a running service never keeps the installer from replacing the installed `hive.exe`.

   On Windows the process gets `CREATE_NO_WINDOW`, and it is killed when the app drops the connection.
2. The bridge connects to `<runtime>/hive.sock`, only when the runtime dir is ours (owned by the user, mode 0700, not a symlink) and the socket's peer runs as the user (`peer_cred`): anything else may be another user's (9.7, `Paths::connect`, also used by `hive hook`).
3. If the connection fails, the bridge prepares the runtime dir, renames the previous `daemon.log` to `daemon.log.1` and opens a new one (0600) and runs `setsid --fork hive daemon` (`hive daemon --remote` from `hive bridge --remote`, a remote service: see `hive::terminal`). The daemon's stdin and stdout are null and its stderr goes to the log.
4. The bridge retries the connection for up to 5 s. If the daemon never listens, the bridge fails with "the hive service did not start; see <log>".
5. `hive bridge --replace` (26.17, Q8, a remote's restart) first ends the running service: the service writes its process id into the lockfile once it holds it (again when it takes a lock put back, `daemon::claim`), and the bridge, finding the lock held, sends that process SIGTERM and waits up to 15 s for the lock to be free (no lockfile, a free lock: nothing to end; a lockfile naming no process: an error). Then it goes on as above and starts its own service.
6. Two bridges racing is harmless: the second daemon cannot take the lockfile and exits. A daemon tries the lockfile again for up to 5 s (the bridge's wait), because a service that is ending keeps it until its terminals have ended (up to their 2 s grace); that service drops its listener and removes the socket as soon as it stops serving, so a bridge started meanwhile starts the new service.
7. The bridge then copies bytes in both directions without looking at them. When either side closes, the bridge exits.

### App connect (app side)
1. The UI calls `connect(onMessage)` at startup (`connect` in `src/connect.ts`, called by `src/main.tsx`), handing a `Channel` to Rust. "Reconnect" goes through the same function, so every connection has the same handler.
2. Rust starts the bridge and queues `hello {role: app, version}`; `version` is the app's `CARGO_PKG_VERSION`, so `hive-app` and `hive` share one version number.
3. `welcome` or `version_mismatch` goes to the UI. After `version_mismatch`, Rust drops the bridge and sends nothing more.
   The UI (`src/shell/ConnectionBlock.tsx`) then blocks the workspace (`inert`, with a modal `alertdialog`; the title bar stays usable) and shows both versions and the fix: `pkill -f 'hive daemon'`, because a refused handshake leaves the old service running (the installed app brings its own `hive`), then, when `bundled`, "reinstall Hive" if it keeps happening, else (a development build) `cargo install --path crates/hive` from the same commit. `disconnected` blocks the same way and shows the reason, then "reinstall Hive" when `bundled`, else a check that `hive` is installed (`cargo install --path crates/hive`). An installed app never shows `cargo`. The dialog's "Reconnect" calls `connect` again, which starts a new bridge.
4. A UI that reloads calls `connect` again: if the connection is up, Rust closes that UI's old terminals, drops their later messages, replays `welcome` and sends `get_settings`, `list_projects` and `resync` for it; otherwise it starts a new bridge.
   A WebContent crash (18.21, D18.5; macOS: WebKit kills the page's process past its memory limit) keeps the terminals and the agents in them: `main.rs` installs Tauri's `on_web_content_process_terminate` (wry's `webViewWebContentProcessDidTerminate:`), whose `web_content_terminated` marks the link (`Hive::web_content_terminated`) and reloads the page. That page's `connect` then closes nothing and, after `welcome`, sends `terminal_kept` for each open terminal; the UI's `reattachTerminal` (`src/terminals.ts`, from `connect.ts`) makes a new Terminal and tab and calls `attach_terminal {id, onData}`, which gives Rust's terminal the new output `Channel` and sends `ack {bytes: 4294967295}`, so output the dead page never acknowledged no longer holds the PTY. The scrollback is not replayed (the local service keeps none): the terminal is redrawn (a resize one column wider and back, 26.16) and resized again once its pane is fitted, so a full-screen program (Claude Code) draws again, while a shell shows its next output only. Output that arrived between the crash and the attach is lost; badges and OSC progress come back only when sent again. Only the next `connect` is marked: any later reload closes the terminals as before.
5. When the bridge's stdout closes, Rust waits up to 2 s for its stderr, sends `terminal_exited` for every open terminal and then `disconnected {reason}`. Commands then fail with "not connected to the hive service".
6. A command that fails is shown, never swallowed (9.21): every command but `connect`, `open_terminal`, `attach_terminal` and `save_file` goes through `send` (`src/transport/tauri.ts`), which shows the error text as an error toast (`showFailure` in `src/store.ts`, 10.3); its promise still rejects, already handled, so a caller that set a pending state ("Removing…", "Restarting…") ends it with `.catch`. `openTerminal` and the terminal openers built on it (`openClaude`, `openWith`, `splitTerminal`, `reattachTerminal`) reject; a click or key hands them to `showOpenFailure` (`src/terminals.ts`). The Biome plugin `biome/no-unshown-failures.grit` refuses `void` on any of these, and a test in `tauri.test.ts` fails on a new command sent without `send`. Toasts (10.3): the store keeps `notices` (`{ id, kind, text }`, at most 3, the newest replacing the oldest) through `showNotice`, `dismissNotice` and, in `reduce`, `withNotice`; `Toasts` shows them bottom-right above the status bar, which shows no message. An `error` stays until its × is clicked; an `info` (a confirmation: "Copied …", `pull_done`, `run_done`) fades after 4 s.

### Remote hosts over ssh (26.17)

Design: `docs/spike/ssh.md` §6.1–6.2 and the human's answers Q4–Q8, Q12; `src-tauri/src/remote.rs`.

1. **One link per host.** The app keeps the local service's link and one per alias of the local service's `remote.hosts` (`Remotes::sync`, on every `settings` from the local service: a new alias starts a link, one gone ends it with `host_link {state: removed}`; an invalid alias is never linked). App commands that go to a service take an optional `host` (absent: the local service; `Hive::on`); a terminal's commands find its link by its id. Terminal ids are shared by every link (`Link::ids`): a new terminal takes the next id of all, skipping any channel its link already uses (a remote's kept terminal keeps its service's channel), so the UI's ids never clash. A remote's messages carry `host`; its `editor_target` and `session_located` never approve a path for `open_path` (the path is on another machine) and its `file` gets no `hive-file:` URL.
   - **What of a remote's reaches the UI** (`REMOTE_TO_UI` in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`, 26.18): what the UI applies to that host's data only (see [Hosts in the UI](#hosts-in-the-ui-2618)): its terminals' (`terminal_opened`, `terminal_exited`, `error`, `badge`, `command_finished`, `terminal_progress`, `unhooked_agent`, `terminal_notification`), its agents' (`agent_*`, `subagent_worktrees`, `session_usage`), its spaces, projects, worktrees, sessions, files and GitHub answers, and its own Claude accounts (`claude_logins`, 27.11), on any channel. Everything else is dropped before the pump acts on it: `settings`, `settings_failed`, `diagnostics`, `shared`, the uninstall's, `restore_sessions`, `session_moved`, `notice`, `usage_alert`, `editor_target` and `session_located` (which approve a local path for `open_path`), `file_range`, and any type only the app sends, such as `files_dropped`, `menu`, `quit_requested`, `uninstalled`, `notification_clicked` or `tray_clicked`. A remote's `file` never gets a `hive-file:` URL and loses a `media.url` it wrote (its images and PDFs do not show). The link still acts on its own `welcome`, `terminals_kept`, `terminal_reset`, `terminal_exited` and `version_mismatch`; the settings and an uninstall come from the local service only (`Link::follow`), and a remote link's agents at risk count for quitting (`Remotes::at_risk`). A remote's `terminal_opened` and `terminal_kept` carry its own `worktree` and `account`, which the UI places on that host only. A reloaded UI gets each remote's `host_link` state and kept terminals (`Link::replay_terminals`), and a linked remote is asked again for its projects and live agents (`list_projects`, `resync`).
   - Whatever link a session came from, only a session id (`valid_id`: letters, digits and `-`, at most 64, not starting with `-`) is ever typed after `claude --resume`: the service keeps and lists no other, and the UI refuses any other (`isSessionId` in `src/sessions.ts`).
   - An alias holds only ASCII letters, digits and `. _ @ : % [ ] -`, with no leading `-` (`check_host`): `ssh` puts it unquoted into a `ProxyCommand`, `Match exec` or `LocalCommand` (`%h`, `%n`).
2. **The command** (macOS only; `main.rs` gives `/usr/bin/ssh` and the app's resources): `ssh -T -o BatchMode=yes -o StrictHostKeyChecking=yes -o ClearAllForwardings=yes -o ForwardAgent=no -o ForwardX11=no -o ForwardX11Trusted=no -o ServerAliveInterval=10 -o ServerAliveCountMax=3 -o ConnectTimeout=15 -- <alias> <command>`, separate arguments, the alias the only one that varies. There is no `ControlMaster` option (Q12); the rest comes from the user's `~/.ssh/config`. The remote command is a constant, `exec /bin/sh -c '<script>' hive <version>`, one line with no `'`, `\` or `!`, so any login shell (bash, zsh, fish, csh) reads it alike.
3. **Check and install.** The run script runs `~/.hive/bin/hive bridge --remote` (`$HIVE_HOME/bin` when absolute) when its `--version` is `hive <version>`, else prints `hive-needs <uname -s> <uname -m>` on stderr and exits 3. `Linux x86_64` or `Linux aarch64` gets the bundled `hive-linux-<cpu>` (26.13) through the upload script: stdin is `<byte count> <sha256>\n` and the bytes; with `umask 077` it writes `hive.new` in the bin folder (made 0700 after `mkdir -p`, so an existing one others can read is fixed, and one the user does not own fails), checks the byte count and the SHA-256 (`sha256sum`) before the file ever runs, then `chmod 755` and its `--version`, and only then renames it over `hive`; a failed check removes it and prints `hive-upload: <why>`. The link then tries again; a host that still needs it stops (`install`). Any other OS or CPU is refused with its `uname` text (cut to 64 characters, control characters as spaces, shown as text) and nothing is uploaded (Q5).
4. **Handshake.** A remote `hello` carries `keep_secs` (`remote.keep_minutes` × 60) and `app_id` (random per launch). A remote's `version_mismatch` is its link's state (`disconnected {kind: version}`), not the local mismatch dialog; `restart_remote` makes the next try run the script with `--replace` (`hive bridge --remote --replace`), which trusts the lockfile only in a runtime folder of the user's own (`check_runtime`) and never through a link (`O_NOFOLLOW`). A local `version_mismatch` ends its connection's terminals (`terminal_exited {code: null}`) and keeps nothing of it.
5. **Failures**, from `ssh`'s stderr (at most 16 KiB): "Host key verification failed" or "REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED" → `host_key` ("run `ssh <alias>` once in a terminal to check and accept its key"; Hive never adds an unknown host to `known_hosts`, Q6; OpenSSH's `UpdateHostKeys` may still add new keys of a host already trusted); "Permission denied" → `auth` ("add the key to your ssh agent"); `hive-needs` → install or `platform`; anything else (the first line) → `network`.
6. **Reconnect** (Q7): a link that drops keeps its terminals (their tabs stay), and a `network` failure waits 1, 2, 5, 10, 30 s, then every 30 s while the link keeps terminals; past the ladder without any it stops (`disconnected {kind: network}`). `host_key`, `auth`, `platform`, `install` and `version` are never retried. `reconnect_host` tries again at once, whatever stopped it; waking from sleep (`NSWorkspaceDidWakeNotification`, `macos::observe_wake` → `woke`) ends the wait of every link not stopped by such a failure; neither does anything while a link is up. On `welcome` the link's kept terminals reattach (26.16, see [Reattach after a dropped link](#reattach-after-a-dropped-link-2616) item 3): each one the service's `terminals_kept` lists is attached again, and each it does not (or a first message that is not `terminals_kept`) gets `terminal_exited {code: null}`. The app's exit ends every remote link (its `ssh` killed), so the remote service keeps its terminals for the keep time, unless the quit dialog's `bye_hosts` ended them (26.18).
7. **A remote cannot harm the app** (26.19, spike §8; `src-tauri/src/limits.rs`). A remote is someone else's machine, so its link has caps the local service's has not; past one the link is dropped at once (its `ssh` killed) and stops with `disconnected {kind: limit}`, "Hive dropped the link to <alias>: it <what it did>.", never retried (only `reconnect_host` tries again):
   - more than 1000 control or data frames, or 16 MiB of them, in a second (`limits::Rate`; terminal output is not counted), or more than 20 messages that show an OS notification (`command_finished`, `terminal_notification`, `usage_alert`, an `agent_state` with `notify`) in 10 seconds;
   - a message the UI takes (`REMOTE_TO_UI`) that its type (`Control`) cannot read, such as a badge's `text` that is an object (`limits::typed`); what it does read is written again as its type, so a field the type has not never reaches the UI;
   - a `projects` of more than 1000 projects, a `sessions` of more than 1000 sessions, a `commits` of more than 1000 commits (27.2), or any list of more than 50,000 items at any depth of any message (`limits::check`, before the allowlist, so a type 26.18 lets through is capped too);
   - more than 16 MiB of one terminal's output the UI has not acknowledged (`Term::unacked`, less each `ack_terminal`, reset when a UI takes the terminal; the service stops at 512 KiB and a read), or 64 MiB of all of the link's terminals;
   - more than 1024 frames, or 64 MiB, waiting to be written to a remote that stopped reading (`limits::Queue`, counted by the link's writer): the request that did not fit fails with "<alias> stopped reading what the app sends".

   A frame over 4 MiB or of an unknown type breaks the stream as before (the codec): a `network` failure, retried, its error as the reason. A remote's titles, texts, messages, errors, reasons, activities, names, accounts, subjects and authors (27.2), and a `terminal_notification`'s body, are cut at 1024 characters with `…` before anything reads them. A remote's `file` (should the allowlist let one through) gets no `hive-file:` URL and keeps none it wrote; a local one keeps only the app's. An OS notification's title is cut at 256 characters and its body at 1024, whatever link it came from. The set of agents that make quitting ask (`Link::at_risk`) holds at most 1024, with ids of at most 256 bytes, on every link. Nothing a remote names is ever opened or revealed: `open_in_editor`, `locate_session`, `open_settings_file` and `open_shared` to a remote host are refused in Rust ("Hive opens nothing of the remote host <alias>") and nothing is sent, and its `editor_target` and `session_located` never approve a path, whatever `REMOTE_TO_UI` lets through. URLs open only through the app command `open_url {url, markdown}` (the webview has no opener permission): an `https://` URL with a host, for every link (a pull request's, a run's, the menu's); a link in Markdown or a terminal (`markdown`, #43, 8.20) may also be `http://` or a `mailto:` address, its query dropped; never `file:`, `javascript:` or another scheme.

### Hosts in the UI (26.18)

Design: `docs/spike/ssh.md` §5 and the human's answers Q1–Q3. A space belongs to one host; the local service is the host "this Mac".

1. **The store keeps hosts apart** (`src/store.ts`, `src/hosts.ts`). `HiveState.host` is the host shown (null: this Mac), the current space's; the service data of `HOST_FIELDS` (spaces, projects, agents, sessions, files, answers) is that host's, and so are its open files (their tabs, edit buffers, table asks and pending saves, the review comments, the trees' folders and the pairs), so a file request or save only ever goes to the file's own host and another host's answer, rename or deletion naming the same path never reaches it; and every other host's is kept in `stash` by host (`hostKey`: "" for this Mac) until shown (`switchedHost`, `showHost`). `apply` (`src/reduce.ts`) applies a message to its `host`'s data, the shown one's or the stash's (`hostView`), so a host's paths and ids never meet another's; of a host not shown, only the terminals, the toasts and the bell's seen agents change (`SHARED_FIELDS`); a remote's toast names its host ("gpu-box: <why>", `named`). Showing another host closes any dialog, question or menu, and a question runs only on the host it was asked on (`Question.host`). A remote's agents are keyed `<host> <id>` (`agentKey`, by `keyed` in `onMessage`), never a local session id. The settings, the connection, the terminals, the tabs and the inbox are the app's. `onMessage` (`src/connect.ts`) takes of a remote only `REMOTE_TYPES` (equal to `REMOTE_TO_UI`, checked by `src/hosts.test.ts`) and its link's `host_link`, `terminal_kept` and `terminal_reattached` (`taken`): none of the local service's handlers ever acts on a remote's message.
2. **Requests go to the shown host.** The Tauri transport adds the shown host as `host` to every request about spaces, projects, worktrees, files, sessions and GitHub (`hosted` in `src/transport/tauri.ts`) and to `open_terminal`; the settings, accounts, diagnostics, uninstall, updates and notifications are this Mac's. A terminal's own requests find its link by its id.
3. **Terminals.** A terminal and its tab carry the host they run on (`Terminal.host`, `Tab.host`: the host shown when opened, or `terminal_kept`'s). `tabWorktree` places another host's tab at a place no local path has (its cwd or worktree prefixed with NUL and its host), and the tab bar lists the shown host's tabs only (`visibleTabs`), so a remote naming a local worktree, or with a local worktree's path as its cwd, never lands in it.
4. **The space switcher** (`src/shell/SpaceSwitcher.tsx`, 27.8) names the space and, for a remote's, its host ("Default · gpu-box"); its list has a section per host with a link (`spaceChoices`, `hostSpaces`), this Mac first, each headed by its name and its link's dot; a down host's spaces show dimmed and cannot be picked. With more than `MANY_HOSTS` (5) remotes (`switcherSections`), a filter on top (space or host name), the shown host's section first and open, the others collapsed to their header until opened (click, →/←) or matched. Picking a space shows its host, then asks its service for that space. One "New space…": its dialog asks where (`HostPicker`: this Mac or a host whose link is up, filtered with many hosts), and picking a host shows it with the dialog left open, so the space and its GitHub accounts are that host's; "Edit space…" edits the current one.
5. **Link state** (`src/shell/Hosts.tsx`, 27.8). With no host the status bar shows "This Mac" and its connection; with hosts one item sums them up (`hostsSummary`): "3 hosts", "1 of 3 hosts down" in the warning colour when a link is reconnecting or disconnected (down, mismatched or refused), "1 of 3 hosts connecting", or this Mac's own state while its service is not connected. A click opens a popover (`HostsPopover`) of every host, the ones with a problem first, then this Mac and the others: its state ("reconnecting since 12s", "disconnected: <reason>"), "shown" for the shown host, Reconnect while down (`reconnect_host`), for another version (Q8) "Restart the remote service" (`restart_remote`), its title the terminals it ends, a filter with many hosts, and "Hosts settings…" (Settings opened on Hosts, `settingsSection`); ↑/↓ move through its field and buttons, Esc or a click outside close it. While the shown host is down the sidebar shows its last tree dimmed under "Reconnecting to <host>…" and Reconnect, and the terminal area "Reconnecting to <host>…" over its terminals, which take no input (`linkDown`) and keep their buffers.
6. **Naming the host.** An alert of a remote's agent names its space and host ("Train · gpu-box", `spaceName`), a terminal's "<worktree> on <host>" (`terminalName`); the bell, F8, the Dock and the menu bar icon count every host's pending agents (`everyHost`); going to one shows its host first. After a reattach (`connected` again after the link was up once), the host's alerts wait `SUMMARY_MS` (1.5 s) and one summary replaces them: "2 agents waiting on gpu-box", the agents pending now that were not pending in that state before the drop (an inbox item showing the host, a tone, a notification); none when nothing new waits.
7. **Quit** (Q2). With agents at risk on a connected host (`remoteAgents`), quitting asks too (the app side counts them: `Remotes::at_risk`), with "Keep N agents running on <host>?" ticked per host; the answers go together as `bye_hosts {byes: [[host, end], …]}` (`bye {end}` to each; the command sends every goodbye, then waits once, up to 2 s, for those services to close their links, so the exit never kills `ssh` before they read it). With `confirm_close` off nothing is asked and the agents are kept.
8. **Off in a remote space** (`onRemote`): Open with Default App and Reveal (the file and worktree menus), the file view's external editor, the session menu's Open Log, Reveal Log and Open Working Directory, Open settings file, the shared folder's Open folder, and the dev-server preview are hidden. A drop from Finder on a remote's terminal types nothing and shows a toast.
9. **Settings → Hosts** (only where the app offers no WSL): each host of `remote.hosts` with its link's state, Test (`reconnect_host`) and Remove; Add takes an alias typed in (Hive does not read `~/.ssh/config`; the service checks it); "Keep remote terminals (minutes)", 1 to 10080. With many hosts the list scrolls and a filter narrows it.
10. **Mock transport** (`?mock=remote`, `src/transport/mockHosts.ts`): this Mac's fake service and a second one standing in for `gpu-box` (project `/home/dev/models`), linked as the app side links a remote, its messages tagged and filtered by `REMOTE_TYPES`. Typed in a terminal, `drop-link` drops the link (reconnecting, until Reconnect, which reattaches its terminals) and `stop-link` stops it (`network`); a host added in the settings that is not `gpu-box` stops with ssh's "Could not resolve hostname". `&hosts=<n>` (27.8) adds fake hosts `build-02` up to n (`mockLink`, `mockSpaces`): connected, but every few one reconnecting, of another version or refused, each with its spaces and serving nothing else; Reconnect or a restart links one.

### Terminal from the UI
1. `openTerminal(cwd, cols, rows, onData)`: Rust picks the next channel (never reused while a connection lasts; numbering starts again once one ends and no terminal is left), registers `onData` and sends `open_terminal` on it. The call resolves with the channel id.
2. Output bytes for that channel go only to its `onData`. Control messages for channels the UI did not open are dropped.
3. `writeTerminal` sends terminal frames, split at `MAX_PAYLOAD`; `resizeTerminal`, `closeTerminal` and `ackTerminal` send `resize`, `close_terminal` and `ack`.
4. `terminal_exited` releases the channel's `onData`.

### Terminals in the UI (xterm.js)
`src/terminals.ts` owns every xterm.js `Terminal`, outside React (#30); output goes straight from the transport's `onData` into `term.write`, never through React state.
1. `openTerminal(cwd)` creates the `Terminal` first, then calls the transport at 80×24, so output that arrives before the tab renders is kept. It adds a tab to the store (`tabs`, `activeTab`, `selection` = the worktree path); the tab's title is the worktree's name from `projects` (the project's name is added when two tabs share a name; an unknown path shows as is).
2. `TerminalArea` renders one host element (`mountTerminals`) and calls `showTerminal(activeTab)`. Only the shown terminal is opened in the DOM, fitted (`FitAddon`) and rendered with the WebGL addon; a hidden one loses its WebGL addon and keeps parsing output into its buffer. A lost WebGL context disposes the addon (xterm falls back to its DOM renderer); WebGL is tried again the next time the terminal is shown.
3. Host size changes are debounced (50 ms) into one fit of the shown terminal; a new size sends `resizeTerminal`.
4. Keys: `interceptKeys(handler)` sees every key event first and keeps the app shortcuts from the terminal (see Shortcuts); then Ctrl+Shift+C copies the selection and Ctrl+Shift+V pastes through the clipboard API (#35; on macOS Cmd+C, with nothing selected Ctrl+C's interrupt (18.2), and Cmd+V, WebKit's own paste (below), and Ctrl+C/Ctrl+Shift+V reach the shell); on macOS (17.14) ⌘← / ⌘→ / ⌘⌫ send `\x01` / `\x05` / `\x15` and ⌘K clears the terminal; everything else goes to xterm and, as `onData`, to `writeTerminal`. Input stops once the terminal exited. Option-drag forces a selection (`macOptionClickForcesSelection`). ⌘= / ⌘- / ⌘0 (`zoomTerminals`) add a zoom to every terminal's font size, within 8–32, kept in the view only (⌘0 or a restart drops it).
   The terminal's own menu (17.20, `TerminalPaneMenu` in `src/shell/TerminalArea.tsx`): a right click (a two-finger tap on a Mac) in a pane (`data-terminal` holds its id; `terminalAt`) opens Hive's menu instead of the WebView's (#53): Copy (disabled with no selection), Paste, Clear and Split right / Unsplit, with their keys; for a pane of a pair, "Replace with ▸" (the worktree's other tabs, terminals and files, 25.1) and "Swap sides" (20.5), as in its tab's menu. A file tab and a file pane's header have the same menu (`FileTabMenu`). `terminalAction` copies and pastes as Ctrl+Shift+C / Ctrl+Shift+V do and clears as ⌘K does, then focuses the terminal. Paste reads the clipboard from script, so WKWebView asks first with its "Paste" bubble when another app filled it; a refused read pastes nothing.
   Files dropped from Finder (18.1, macOS; reverses D17.4's default): a file or folder dropped on a terminal pane types its path there, as Terminal.app and iTerm2 do (Claude Code turns a pasted image path into an attachment). Tauri's own drag and drop stays off (`dragDropEnabled: false`): wry's handler answers every drag of the webview (`wry/src/wkwebview/drag_drop.rs`: a handled drag never reaches `super`, and Tauri's handler always answers handled), and on macOS WebKit's HTML5 drags inside the page (tabs, the sidebar's agents, the Files tree) go through the same AppKit `NSDraggingDestination` methods of the same view, so turning it on would end them. Instead `src-tauri/src/macos.rs` (`take_file_drops`, through the Objective-C runtime, no crate) replaces only the webview class's `performDragOperation:`: a drop with no `draggingSource` (from another app) carrying file paths (`NSFilenamesPboardType`, at most 1000 paths of at most 4096 bytes) is taken (WebKit gets `draggingExited:` instead, so the page never opens the file), and every other drop goes on to the class's own method. A string's length is checked (`lengthOfBytesUsingEncoding:`) before it is copied out, and only paths `quote::usable` accepts are kept: absolute, with no control character. The paths go to `Hive::files_dropped` with the drop point in the view's points from its top left, which sends `files_dropped {text, x, y}`; `text` is `quote::typed` (`src-tauri/src/quote.rs`, also for 18.3's paste of copied files): each path as one word bash, zsh and fish read back unchanged (a backslash before each shell character, as Terminal.app; non-ASCII as is), each followed by a space. A control character (C0, DEL, C1) is never typed, quoted or not: the line editor acts on it as a key before any shell parses it (an escape ends the bracketed paste, a newline presses Enter), so a path holding one, or a relative path, is left out, and a text past 64 KiB (`TYPED_LIMIT`) types nothing. `dropFiles` refuses a text with a control character too. The UI (`dropFiles` in `src/terminals.ts`, from `connect.ts`) finds the pane at the point as the menu does (`document.elementFromPoint` → `terminalAt`) and pastes the text there (`term.paste`: bracketed when the program asked, no Enter), and that terminal takes the keyboard; a drop anywhere else (sidebar, tab bar, a dialog over the pane, a file tab) is ignored. In the mock transport `drop-files <x> <y> <text>` typed in a terminal stands in for a drop.
   ⌘V (18.3, macOS) stays WebKit's own paste (the Edit menu's Paste answers the key the page leaves alone, so no "Paste" bubble), which fires a trusted `paste` event on xterm's textarea; in WKWebView text arrives as its plain text (rich text included) and xterm pastes it, line breaks as CR, bracketed when the program asked. `onPaste` (`src/terminals.ts`, a capture listener on each pane, before xterm's) always cancels the event's default, which inserted the text into xterm's hidden textarea as well, where it stayed. Files copied in Finder reach a page only as `files` with their names and no text, so xterm sent an empty paste: a shell showed nothing, and Claude Code, which takes an empty paste on macOS as "read the clipboard's image" (`osascript … «class PNGf»`), attached the file's icon. Now, when the event carries files, `onPaste` stops it and asks the app command `pasted_files` (sync, on the main thread): `Hive::pasted_files` reads the clipboard's file paths through the `Clipboard` trait (`macos::Pasteboard::general()`: the general `NSPasteboard`'s `NSFilenamesPboardType`, which macOS gives for Finder's file URLs, with the drop's limits, inside its own autorelease pool), answers `quote::typed` (18.1's filter: a path with a control character is left out, so an escape can never end the bracketed paste early; nothing past 64 KiB). `file_paths` first checks the serialized list's length (`dataForType:`) and parses nothing over `LIST_LIMIT` (1000 × (4096 + 64) bytes), since any app can fill the clipboard; it parses that very data (`NSPropertyListSerialization`), never a second fetch another app could have replaced; the UI pastes that (`term.paste`, no Enter). No files (an image copied, a screenshot sent to the clipboard) answers empty and the event's own text is pasted, empty for an image, so Claude Code reads the image itself, as after its own Ctrl+V or Terminal.app's ⌘V. The terminal menu's Paste (and Ctrl+Shift+V's script read on macOS) asks `pasted_files` too, beside its `readText`. In the mock transport `pastedFiles` answers what `copy-files <text>` typed in a terminal set (`copy-files` alone empties it); elsewhere the app has no clipboard reader and answers empty.
5. `closeTerminal(id)` sends `close_terminal` unless the shell already exited, disposes the `Terminal` and removes the tab (the right neighbour, or the new last tab, is shown). An exited terminal keeps its tab, marked "exited", until closed; `unhooked_agent` only marks the terminal, counted in Settings' About (the tab shows no badge, 24.11).
6. The `terminal` settings (see [Settings](#settings)) and the theme's colors are each terminal's xterm options (`termOptions`), set on new terminals and on every open one when `settings` change (the shown one refits). With `copy_on_select`, a new selection is copied to the clipboard.
7. Each `term.write` of output has a callback: once xterm.js has parsed the bytes they count as written, and every 64 KiB (`ACK_BYTES`, under the service's 128 KiB low mark) `ackTerminal` acknowledges them (9.19, see Flow control in [Wire protocol](#wire-protocol)). Hidden terminals parse their output too, so they keep acknowledging it; an exited one stops. Output that arrives before `openTerminal` resolves is acknowledged with the next batch.
8. Search (17.21): each terminal loads xterm's `SearchAddon`. Ctrl+Shift+F (macOS: ⌘F) sets the store's `terminalSearch` to the active tab (`openTerminalSearch`) and `TerminalSearch` portals its bar into that terminal's pane (`paneOf`) while it is the tab shown. The bar keeps its text and case toggle as UI state and calls `searchTerminal` (up from the newest output, or down with `newer`; `restart` forgets the search, since the addon would keep its old matches for a changed case alone); `onSearchResults` feeds its count. Esc (`endSearch`) clears the highlights and focuses the terminal, keeping the match selected; a bar hidden by another tab clears its highlights.

### Shortcuts (#35)
`src/shortcuts.ts` has one `keydown` listener on the window and one command table, `COMMANDS` (id, label, keys, action): `shortcut()` runs the command whose keys were pressed, and the settings' Shortcuts section lists them. A focused terminal gives each key to `interceptKeys` first: a shortcut is kept from xterm, which leaves it unhandled, so it bubbles up to the window listener and runs once; every other key (Ctrl+Shift+C/V included) is the terminal's.
1. Ctrl+Shift+T opens the worktree picker (every worktree of every project, filtered by worktree or project name; ↑/↓, Enter or a click opens a terminal in its path; Esc or a click outside closes). Ctrl+Shift+N opens the new worktree dialog for the selected project, the project of the selected worktree (a selected agent counts as its worktree, `selectedPlace` in `src/store.ts`; the tab bar's "+" uses it too), or the first project (add project when there is none). Ctrl+Shift+B shows or hides the side panel's view, as its X does; the rail of view icons stays (26.12). Ctrl+Shift+O opens add project. Ctrl+, opens the settings. Ctrl+Shift+P opens the command palette (`src/shell/Palette.tsx`): one list, filtered by a subsequence match ranked by runs and word starts, grouped as Commands (every `COMMANDS` entry with its keys, plus `paletteCommands` extras such as "Remove merged worktrees…", "Run: <name>" for each run script of the selected worktree's project and "Send review"), Agents and worktrees (agents of every space, the current space's worktrees; Enter goes to the agent as F8 does, `goToAgent`, or selects the worktree and expands its project) and Files (the lines of the files panel's worktree holding the text, `search_files` once typing pauses; a row opens the file at that line as the Files panel does). F8 (no modifiers, or a click on the "N pending" counter) selects the pending agent after the selected one (else after the one whose terminal is shown) in tree order, wrapping; it expands the agent's project and worktree, shows its terminal when it has a tab and scrolls it into view. Agents outside every project come last. With nothing pending it does nothing.
2. On macOS every Ctrl+Shift+letter shortcut is Cmd+Shift+letter instead (and Ctrl+, is Cmd+,) (`commandKey` in `src/window.ts`, from the user agent); Ctrl+Shift+letter then goes to the terminal. Tooltips and kbd chips show `⇧⌘B` there (`keyText`). macOS also has its own commands (`mac` in `COMMANDS`, D17.2, 17.14): ⇧⌘J next pending (beside F8, a command's `macKeys`), ⌘W closes the tab shown (as its ×), ⌘1–9 show the bar's tab, ⇧⌘] / ⇧⌘[ the next / previous one (wrapping), ⌘= / ⌘- / ⌘0 the terminal font zoom; and (18.34, D18.10) ⌘T a terminal in the selected worktree (`newTerminal`, the picker with none), ⌘N new worktree (`macKeys`), ⌃Tab / ⌃⇧Tab next / previous tab (`macKeys`: `Control` in a command's keys is the Control key itself, shown ⌃). The terminal takes Fn + an arrow (PageUp/PageDown/Home/End) to scroll its scrollback outside the alternate screen (`macScroll` in `terminals.ts`), and a trackpad pinch on the terminals' host (a Ctrl+wheel in Chromium, gesture events in WebKit, `onPinch`) is ⌘= / ⌘-, one step per event. A key whose character Shift or the layout changes (`[ ] = -` and digits) also matches by its physical key (`event.code`). Enter in the palette, the worktree picker and the review comment (Esc too) is ignored while it ends a composition (`composing`: `isComposing`, or keyCode 229, WebKit's Enter after `compositionend`).
3. Nothing runs under the connection block (`version_mismatch`, `disconnected`) or while a dialog is open; the key then goes on as usual.
4. The tree (sidebar): ↑/↓ move between rows, ←/→ collapse and expand a project or a worktree with agents, Enter selects (rows are buttons).

### Closing the app (#18)
1. The title bar Close, Alt+F4 and the taskbar all become one Tauri close request. `guardClose` (`src/window.ts`) listens to it with `onCloseRequested` and asks `confirmClose` (`src/shell/CloseAppDialog.tsx`).
2. When `agentsAtRisk` is empty, or the `agents.confirm_close` setting is off, the window is destroyed at once. Otherwise the request is cancelled and the "Close Hive?" dialog opens: Cancel/Esc keeps the app, "Close Hive" (focused, Enter) calls `closeWindow`, which destroys the window without asking again. Stage 1 counts every detected agent; Stage 2 narrows `agentsAtRisk` to working, waiting for permission and waiting for you.
3. The last window gone, Tauri emits `RunEvent::Exit`; `on_run_event` runs `Hive::shutdown`: the frame queue closes, so the bridge's stdin closes, the bridge exits and the daemon runs "App disconnect" below. Shutdown waits up to 2 s for the bridge to end, then kills it (no `wsl.exe` left behind). A crashed app closes the same pipe through the OS.
